\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}} }}

Figure 14.
Adjustment #2, in which 2 values adjacent to
x
become
\mathbf{e}_{1}
. In the picture, the shaded squares are pixels with value
\mathbf{e}_{1}
under
\hat{f}
, and the white squares have value different from
\mathbf{e}_{1}
(but not equal to
-\mathbf{e}_{1}
). In the picture we use
k=5
.

Applying these two adjustments in all applicable locations produces a map
\hat{f}^{\prime}\colon(I_{km+k-1,kn+k-1},\partial I_{km,kn})\to(S^{2},-\mathbf{e}_{1})
. Since each of these adjustments may evidently be effected by a (short) sequence of spider moves, we have
\hat{f}^{\prime}\simeq\hat{f}
.

The third step of our construction is to consider
g=\hat{f}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{e}_{2},\mathbf{e}_{3},-\mathbf{e}_{1}}
. This is the iterated flood of
\hat{f}^{\prime}
with
\mathbf{e}_{2}
followed by
\mathbf{e}_{3}
followed by
-\mathbf{e}_{1}
. By Lemma
6.6
we will have
\hat{f}^{\prime}\approx\hat{f}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{e}_{2},\mathbf{e}_{3},-\mathbf{e}_{1}}
, and thus
g\approx f
as desired. It remains to show that the values
x
where
g(x)=\mathbf{e}_{1}
are separated from one another.

The set
S
of points
x
with
\hat{f}^{\prime}(x)=\mathbf{e}_{1}
consists of a (possibly disconnected) union of
k\times k
blocks of points, together with some extra points added by the two adjustments. This set looks something like Figure
15
, in which
k=5
.

\star
\star
\star
\star
\star
\star
\star
\square
\square
\square
\square

Figure 15.
Possible arrangement of points with
\hat{f}^{\prime}(x)=\mathbf{e}_{1}
. Points marked with
\square
are “adjusted corner” points, which may be adjacent to other points with two different labels. Points marked with
\star
are “exterior corner” points, which may be adjacent to other points with 3 different labels. All other points are adjacent to 0 or 1 labels other than
\mathbf{e}_{1}
. In the picture we use
k=5
.

This set
S
includes two types of special points: the “exterior corner points”, lying on corners of the
k\times k
blocks which are not adjacent to other
k\times k
blocks in
S
, and the “adjusted corner points”, the points created by Adjustment #2 which are adjacent to the neighboring
k\times k
block. We label these points with
\star
and
\square
respectively in Figure
15
. We will show that these exterior and adjusted corner points are the only points which can be labeled
\mathbf{e}_{1}
by
g
. To do this, we show that all other points
x\in S
have
g(x)\neq\mathbf{e}_{1}
. Since the exterior and adjusted corner points are never adjacent, and will be separated from each other by at least
k-2
points in a horizontal or vertical direction, this will complete the proof.

Let
x\in S
be some point which is not an exterior or adjusted corner of
S
. We consider cases according to the number of different values which can occur for points
\hat{f}^{\prime}(y)
with
y\sim x
. By our construction, since
x
is not an exterior or adjusted corner, it will be adjacent to at most 1 other label different from
\mathbf{e}_{1}
.

If all points
y\sim x
have the same value
\hat{f}^{\prime}(y)=\mathbf{e}_{1}
, then
\hat{f}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{e}_{2}}(x)=\mathbf{e}_{2}
by the definition of the flood. Since no subsequent flood by
\mathbf{e}_{3}
or
-\mathbf{e}_{1}
can cause the label of
x
to become
\mathbf{e}_{1}
, we see that
g(x)=\hat{f}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{e}_{2},\mathbf{e}_{3},-\mathbf{e}_{1}}(x)\neq\mathbf{e}_{1}
as desired.

Our second case is when
x
is adjacent to only 1 label other than
\mathbf{e}_{1}
. That is, there is some
a\in S^{2}
such that all points
y\sim x
have either the value
\hat{f}^{\prime}(y)=\mathbf{e}_{1}
or
\hat{f}^{\prime}(y)=a
. If
a\neq-\mathbf{e}_{2}
, then
\hat{f}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{e}_{2}}(x)=\mathbf{e}_{2}
and so as above we will have
g(x)\neq\mathbf{e}_{1}
as desired. If
a=-\mathbf{e}_{2}
, then the flood by
\mathbf{e}_{2}
will not change the label on
x
, so
\hat{f}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{e}_{2}}(x)=\mathbf{e}_{1}
. But the subsequent flood by
\mathbf{e}_{3}
will change the value to
\mathbf{e}_{3}
, giving
\hat{f}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{e}_{2},\mathbf{e}_{3}}(x)=\mathbf{e}_{3}
. Again as above this means that
g(x)\neq\mathbf{e}_{1}
as desired.
∎

Finally, we are ready to complete the proof that
d:\pi_{2}(S^{2},-\mathbf{e}_{1})\to\mathbb{Z}
is an isomorphism.

Proof of Theorem
5.5
.

In Proposition
5.4
and Lemma
5.7
we have already shown that
d:\pi_{2}(S^{2},-\mathbf{e}_{1})\to\mathbb{Z}
is a surjective homomorphism. Here
we show that
\ker d
is trivial. Let
f:(I_{m,n},\partial I_{m,n})\to(S^{2},-\mathbf{e}_{1})
with
d(f)=0
; we will show that
f\in\pi_{2}(S^{2},-\mathbf{e}_{1})
is the trivial element.

By Lemma
6.7
, we may assume—up to extension homotopy—that
f
has only isolated values of
\mathbf{e}_{1}
occuring at the center of
3\times 3
blocks of points, and outside of these
3\times 3
“islands”,
f
is constant with value
-\mathbf{e}_{1}
. (The last flood by
-\mathbf{e}_{1}
in the proof of Lemma
6.7
will achieve the latter.) Furthermore we may assume that these islands are separated from each other by any distance we wish.

Using Lemma
3.6
to apply Theorem
6.5
to each island locally, we may replace each island by the maps
T
, or
T^{-1}
according to their triangle-count, or replace the island entirely by constant values
-\mathbf{e}_{1}
if the triangle count for that island is zero.

Since these islands are separated by arbitrarily large regions of constant values of
-\mathbf{e}_{1}
, by Remark
3.5
we may translate the remaining islands into any configuration we wish. Since
d(f)=0
, the number of islands of type
T
must equal the number of islands of type
T^{-1}
. Say that there are
k
islands of each type. Then we may arrange them all to be stacked diagonally as in Figure
5
(extending the domain if necessary), so that
f\approx kT\cdot kT^{-1}
, and this is the trivial element because
T
and
T^{-1}
are inverses.
∎

7.
Future work

An obvious direction in which to continue is to define, for each
n
, a homotopy group
\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})
for
X
a digital image. We believe this should be a straightforward generalization of the approach taken here, with the group consisting of (suitably defined) extension-homotopy equivalence classes of maps
(I,\partial I)\to(X,x_{0})
with
I=I_{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times I_{m_{n}}
an
n
-fold product of intervals. We believe that most, if not all, of the results through Section
4
should have direct generalizations. One main issue in proceeding with this is simply an expositional one, dealing with the increasingly burdensome notational complexity. It also seems reasonable to extend and generalize the development here to include suitable relative homotopy groups and, if possible, develop the long exact sequence in homotopy groups in this digital context.

In
[
15
]
it was shown that the fundamental group (as defined there) of a 2D digital image is a free group. A reasonable question to ask here is: must
\pi_{2}(X,x_{0})
for
X
a 3D digital image be a free abelian group?
On a related note, we may ask about torsion in
\pi_{2}(X,x_{0})
. For instance, what is an example of a digital image
X
that has
\pi_{2}(X,x_{0})
with non-trivial torsion subgroup? Questions of this sort about
\pi_{1}(X,x_{0})
are effectively resolved in
[
15
]
by the identification of the digital fundamental group with the ordinary fundamental group of the spatial realization of the clique complex of the digital image considered as a graph. Here, then, we can ask whether
\pi_{2}(X,x_{0})
may be identified with the ordinary second homotopy group of the spatial realization of the clique complex of
X
?

In a different direction, the calculation of
\pi_{2}(S^{2},-\mathbf{e}_{1})
given here suggests many points of contact with classical topology. Although our maps
I_{m,n}\to S^{2}
are really just graph homomorphisms, the line of argument is strongly suggestive of topological ingredients such as the degree of a map, the classical homotopy group(s) of a topological space, triangulations, simplicial complexes, polyhedra and so-on. Is it possible to somehow make these connections more precise?
